factorization systems in ∞ \infty -categories
Roman Kositsyn

Abstract
. We extend the notion of a factorization system in a category to the realm of ∞ \infty -categories. To this end, we provide a description of the category of ∞ \infty -categories with factorization systems as the category of presheaves of spaces on a certain category that satisfy a version of the Segal condition. Additionally, we study the notion of distributive laws between monads and, more generally, lax functors in the context of categories with factorization systems. In particular, we also characterize categories with factorization systems as distributive laws in the bicategory of spans.



1.
Introduction

The principal goal of this paper is to present a homotopy-coherent notion of a factorization system suitable for applications to
\infty
-categories. Roughly speaking, a factorization system on an ordinary category
C
is the data of two subcategories
(E,M)
such that every morphism
f
in
C
uniquely decomposes as
f=e\circ m
with
e\in E
and
m\in M
. Examples of factorization systems abound in mathematics, common general sources of them include vertical/cartesian factorization system on Grothendieck fibration and generic/free factorization systems associated to cartesian monads studied in
[
Web04
]
. In the
\infty
-categorical context we also have factorization systems attached to Cartesian fibrations and Cartesian monads (this time using
[
CH19
]
), as well as, for example, factorization systems arising from
t
-structures as described in
[
FL16
]
.

Factorization systems were first defined in
[
Isb57
]
, however numerous equivalent formalizations of the concept have appeared since. The definition closest to ours can be found in
[
FK72
]
. According to op. cit. a factorization system on a category
C
is given by a pair
(E,M)
of classes of morphisms of
C
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

Both
E
and
M
contain all isomorphisms and are closed under composition;

(2)

Every morphism
f
of
C
can be factored as
f=m\circ e
with
m\in M
and
e\in E
;

(3)

The factorization is functorial in the following sense: for any morphism
(u,v)
in the arrow category from
(m,e)
to
(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})
as shown below there is a unique dotted arrow
w
making the diagram commute

Alternatively, a factorization system can be defined by lifting properties as in
[
Bou77
]
or as an algebra for a certain monad on
\mathrm{Cat}
as in
[
KT93
]
. We define a factorization system on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
to be given by a pair of subcategories
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{V}
that satisfy the following condition:

(
*
)

For any morphism
f\in\mathscr{C}
the space of factorizations

\{f\cong h\circ v,h\in\mathscr{H},v\in\mathscr{V}\}

is contractible.

Condition (

∗

‣
1
) certainly implies (
2
) and (
3
) above, but it appears to contradict (
1
) since it requires every isomorphism to admit a unique decomposition as well. However, this is not such a big problem, at least if we restrict ourselves to complete Segal spaces
\mathscr{C}
, since in that case we simply consider all invertible morphisms as elements of the underlying space of objects. Alternative definitions of a factorization system for
\infty
-categories can be found in
[
Lur09
, Definition 5.2.8.8.]
and
[
Joy08
, Section 24]
, the only essential difference between those definitions and ours is that they require
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{V}
to be closed under retracts. Additionally,
[
LV17
]
provides a treatment of factorization systems in the context of triangulated and stable categories.

Despite its simplicity, condition (

∗

‣
1
) is not very useful in practice. To rectify this, we give an alternative description of
\infty
-categories with factorization systems as models for a certain theory
\mathpzc{Fact}
. We use the term ”theory” in the same way as in
[
Kos21
]
, which is itself an adaptation of an earlier paper
[
BMW12
]
to the formalism of
\infty
-categories. Roughly speaking, a theory gives us a way to represent the category of algebras for a certain monad as a category of presheaves satisfying a Segal-type condition. Objects that can be represented this way include Segal spaces, commutative monoids and symmetric operads to name a few. Our first main result is
theorem

2.18
that provides an equivalence between the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories with factorization systems and the
\infty
-category of models for the theory
\mathpzc{Fact}
.

Another object of interest for us is the notion of a distributive law. They were originally defined in
[
Bec69
]
. By definition, a distributive law between monads
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
consists of a natural transformation
\alpha:T_{1}T_{2}\rightarrow T_{2}T_{1}
making the following diagrams commute

The main motivation behind this notion is that it allows us to endow
T_{2}T_{1}
with the structure of a monad with multiplication given by
T_{2}T_{1}T_{2}T_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha}T_{2}T_{2}T_{1}T_{1}\xrightarrow{m_{2}*m_{1}}T_{2}T_{1}
. This definition, of course, cannot be used in the higher-categorical context as it lacks the necessary higher coherences. Instead, we conceptualize this notion as a special case of a ”lax functor” from a category with factorization system. A lax functor from a category
C
to a bicategory
B
(as defined e.g. in
[
Str72b
]
) is the data of a morphism
F:C\rightsquigarrow B
of the underlying graphs of
C
and
B
together with 2-morphisms
\alpha_{f,g}:F(g)\circ F(f)\rightarrow F(g\circ f)
for every composable pair of morphisms in
C
and
\beta_{x}:\mathrm{id}_{F(x)}\rightarrow F(\mathrm{id}_{x})
for every object of
x
that satisfy certain associativity constraints. In particular, giving a lax functor from a point to
B
is equivalent to giving a monad in
B
. The
\infty
-categorical generalization of this concept can be found for example in
[
GHL20
]
,
[
GR17
]
and
[
Kos21
]
. Our extension of this notion to categories with factorization systems is quite technical, but roughly speaking a lax functor from
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathscr{B}
contains all the data associated to the lax functor from the underlying categories
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{H}
to
\mathscr{B}
together with additional 2-morphisms
\gamma_{h,v}:F(v)\circ F(h)\rightarrow F(\widetilde{h})\circ F(\widetilde{v})
for every factorization
v\circ h\cong\widetilde{h}\circ\widetilde{v}
in
\mathscr{F}
. We then define a distributive law to be a lax functor from a singleton category considered as a category with a factorization system. Classical distributive laws can be characterized as monads in the category of monads, and the same is true for our notion of a distributive law by
remark

3.34
, so it follows that it reduces to the usual notion for the case of discrete categories. Distributive laws are not the only interesting case of lax functors, however. Our second main result
theorem

5.7
provides an equivalence between the category of lax functors from an
\infty
-category with a factorization system
\mathscr{F}
to the flagged bicategory of spans and the overcategory
\mathrm{Fact}_{/\mathscr{F}}
in the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories with factorization systems. In particular, the category
\mathrm{Fact}
itself can be identified with the category of distributive laws in spans (since
*
is the terminal object in
\mathrm{Fact}
, so
\mathrm{Fact}_{/*}\cong\mathrm{Fact}
).

We will now briefly go through the contents of each section.
Section

2
contains the description of the the theory
\mathpzc{Fact}
as well as
theorem

2.18
that provides an equivalence between the models of this theory and categories with factorization systems.
Section

3
contains the definition of lax functors from categories with factorization systems. Additionally, it contains the description of bicategories with factorization system in
3.3
as well as an alternative construction of the Gray tensor product. The main result of this section is
3.31
which explicitly describes lax functors with domains lying in
\mathpzc{Fact}
.
Section

4
contains the definition and basic properties of complete categories with factorization systems.
Section

5
contains our second main result
theorem

5.7
providing an equivalence between an overcategory
\mathrm{Fact}_{/\mathscr{F}}
and a certain subcategory of lax functors from
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathrm{Span}
.

Notations and conventions
:

Our standard reference for
\infty
-categories is
[
Lur09
]
, however we will also use the equivalence between quasicategories and complete Segal spaces (see e.g.
[
JT07
]
) without further mention. We will typically call
\infty
-categories simply categories, except in cases where it may lead to confusion. We denote by
\mathscr{S}
the category of spaces and by
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C})
the category of presheaves on a category
\mathscr{C}
. We assume the reader is familiar with the terminology and results of
[
CH19
]
and
[
Kos21
]
. For a theory
\mathscr{O}
we will typically denote
\mathrm{O}
the category
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathscr{O}}(\mathscr{S})
of models for this theory. In particular, we will denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}
the algebraic pattern of Segal spaces (i.e.
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
with the usual active/inert factorization and with elementary objects given by
[0]
and
[1]
) and by
\mathrm{Cat}
the category of Segal spaces. We will also sometimes refer to the object in
\mathrm{O}
as ”presheaves on
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
satisfying the Segal condition”. We use the terms ”
n
-fold Segal space” and ”
n
-category” interchangeably. In particular, a category in our sense does not have to be a complete Segal space, instead we denote by
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp}}
the category of complete Segal spaces.

2.
Theory of categories with factorization systems

Our basic objective in this section is to construct a theory
\mathpzc{Fact}
whose models are given by categories with factorization systems. More specifically, we define it to be the category whose objects are finite strings
S
made of two characters. To such a string we attach a category
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, which can be thought of as a category containing all possible compositions and factorizations of the characters appearing in
S
, and define morphisms in
\mathpzc{Fact}
to be given by morphisms between
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
that preserve the factorization systems. Our first major result is
2.6
, which gives a purely combinatorial description of
\mathpzc{Fact}
, similar to the well-known description of
\Delta
as a category generated by faces and degeneracies. This, in particular, allows us to endow
\mathpzc{Fact}
with the structure of a theory with arities in
2.9
. It also contributes to the proof of our main result
theorem

2.18
that provides an equivalence between the category of models of
\mathpzc{Fact}
and the category of categories with factorization systems.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a category
\mathscr{C}
admits an factorization system if there are subcategories
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{V}
of
\mathscr{C}
with the same underlying space of objects such that for every
f\in\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})
the space of decompositions

\{f\cong h\circ v,h\in\mathscr{H},v\in\mathscr{V}\}

is contractible. We will refer to the subcategory
\mathscr{H}
as the
horizontal
subcategory and to
\mathscr{V}
as the
vertical
subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, we will typically denote morphisms in
\mathscr{H}
by
c\rightharpoonup d
and morphisms in
\mathscr{V}
by
c^{\prime}\rightharpoondown d^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Denote by
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
the category whose objects are given by sequences

S\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(0,1,...,i_{0}|0,1,...,j_{0}|i_{0},i_{0}+1,...i_{1}|j_{0},...,|i_{k},i_{k}+1,...,n|j_{s},j_{s}+1,...,m).

Every sequence like this can be identified with a string of length
(n+m)
containing symbols
h
and
v
, in which the first
i_{0}
symbols are given by
h
, followed by
j_{0}
symbols
v
, followed by
(i_{1}-i_{0})
symbols
h
etc., and so we would often denote the object
S
above by
(i_{0}|j_{0}|i_{1}-i_{0}|j_{1}-j_{0}|...|n-i_{k}|m-j_{k})
. Note that to every object of this form we can associate a string of morphisms in the category
[n]\times[m]
of maximal length.

To each chain
S
we can associate a full subcategory
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
of
[n]\times[m]
that contains all objects that lie above
S
viewed as a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,m)
in
[n]\times[m]
. Observe that every morphism in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
admits a decomposition
\overline{h}\circ\overline{v}
, where
\overline{v}
is a morphism of the form
(\mathrm{id},s)
and
\overline{h}
is a morphism of the form
(t,\mathrm{id})
. We define a morphism from
S
to
S^{\prime}
in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
to be a morphism of categories from
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
to
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
that preserves this factorization system, i.e. that takes vertical morphisms to vertical morphisms and horizontal morphisms to horizontal morphisms.

Example 2.3
.

The diagram below shows an example of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
for
S=(1|1|2|1)
:

Lemma 2.4
.

A morphism
F:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
S\subset\mathrm{Sq}(S)
.

Proof.

Indeed, denote by
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 1}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
containing the objects belonging to
S
as well as those objects that appear in the horizontal/vertical factorization of morphisms of the form
v_{s}\circ h_{t}
, where
v_{s}
(resp.
h_{t}
) is an elementary vertical (resp. horizontal) morphism in
S
(in
2.3
the category
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 1}
contains all objects except
(0,2)
and
(1,2)
). Then since
F
preserves the factorization we see that the value of
F
on
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 1}
is uniquely determined by its value on
S
.

We can then define
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 2}
as the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
containing the factorizations of the elementary morphisms in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 1}
and extend
F
to it, then define
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq 3}
etc. Since
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
is a finite category, we will eventually get
\mathrm{Sq}(S)_{\leq n}\cong\mathrm{Sq}(S)
for some
n
, which concludes the proof of our claim.
∎

Construction 2.5
.

We will describe certain morphism in the category
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
. First, denote by
\sigma^{h}_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq n
the morphism

(0,1,...,i,i+1,...,n|j_{s},...,m)\rightarrow(0,1,...,i,...,n-1|j_{s},...,m)

from the string
S
to the string
S_{\widehat{i}}
obtained from
S
by deleting the
i
th elementary horizontal morphism. The morphism itself is given by sending all elementary morphisms in
S
to the corresponding morphisms in
S_{\widehat{i}}
, except the
i
th horizontal morphism that is sent to the identity. We can also define
\sigma^{v}_{j}
for
0\leq j\leq m
for vertical morphisms.

Similarly, for
0\leq i<n+1
we can define a morphism

(0,1,...,i,i+1,...,n|j_{s},...,m)\rightarrow(0,1,...,i,i+1,i+2...,n+1|j_{s},...,m)

from the string
S
to the string
S_{\widetilde{i}}
obtained from
S
by adding an extra horizontal morphism between
i-1
and
i
. The morphism itself send the string
S
into
S_{\widetilde{i}}
such that the image omits the
i
th object. We can similarly define
\delta^{v}_{j}
for
0<j\leq m+1
.

Observe that the morphisms
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}
and
\delta^{v,h}_{i}
satisfy the usual simplicial identities

(1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta^{v,h}_{i}\circ\delta^{v,h}_{j}\cong\delta^{v,h}_{j+1}\circ\delta^{{v,h}}_{i}\;\text{ if }i\leq j\\
\sigma^{{v,h}}_{j}\circ\sigma^{v,h}_{i}\cong\sigma^{v,h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{v,h}_{j+1}\;\text{ if }i\leq j\\
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}\circ\delta^{v,h}_{i}\cong\begin{cases}\delta^{v,h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{{v,h}}_{j-1}&\text{ if }i<j\\
\mathrm{id}_{S}\;\text{ if }i=j&\text{ or }i=j+1\\
\delta^{v,h}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{{v,h}}_{j}&\text{ if }j+1<i\\
\end{cases}.\end{split}

Apart from that, they also satisfy the following commutativity relations:

(2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma_{j}^{v}\circ\delta_{i}^{h}&\cong\delta^{h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{v}_{j}\\
\sigma_{j}^{h}\circ\delta_{i}^{v}&\cong\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\sigma^{h}_{j}\\
\delta_{j}^{v}\circ\delta_{i}^{h}&\cong\delta^{h}_{i}\circ\delta^{v}_{j}\text{ for $(i,j)\neq(0,0)$ and $(i,j)\neq(n+1,m+1)$}\\
\sigma_{j}^{v}\circ\sigma_{i}^{h}&\cong\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{v}_{j}.\end{split}

Finally, there are morphisms

\gamma_{j,i}:(0,1,...,j|i,...,m)\rightarrow(0,1,...,i|j,...,n)

from a string
S
in which the
j
th vertical morphism is followed by the
i
th horizontal morphism to the string
\widetilde{S}
in which those morphisms switch places. The morphism itself sends all elementary morphisms in
S
to the corresponding morphisms in
\widetilde{S}
, except for the
i
th horizontal and
j
th vertical morphisms that are sent to the horizontal/vertical decomposition of the corresponding morphisms in
\widetilde{S}
. The diagram below illustrates a particular example of this morphism:

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Observe that different
\gamma_{j,i}
commute with each other (in the following formula we implicitly assume that both compositions make sense)

(3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{j,i}\circ\gamma_{k,t}

\displaystyle\cong\gamma_{k,t}\circ\gamma_{j,i}.

Their interactions with various
\sigma_{j}^{v,h}
and
\delta^{v,h}_{i}
can be described by the following relations:

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma_{i}^{h}&\cong\sigma_{i-1}^{h}\circ\gamma_{j,i}\\
\sigma_{j}^{v}&\cong\sigma_{j}^{v}\circ\gamma_{j,i}\\
\sigma_{k}^{h}\circ\gamma_{j,i}&\cong\gamma_{j,i-1}\circ\sigma^{h}_{k}\text{ for $k\neq i$}\\
\sigma_{k}^{v}\circ\gamma_{j,i}&\cong\gamma_{j-1,i}\circ\sigma^{v}_{k}\text{ for $k\neq j$}\\
\gamma_{i+1,j}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}&\cong\delta^{h}_{i}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\text{ for $0<i<n+1$}\\
\gamma_{i,j+1}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\circ\delta^{v}_{j}&\cong\delta^{v}_{j}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\text{ for $0<j<m+1$}\\
\gamma_{i,j}\circ\delta^{h}_{k}&\cong\delta^{h}_{k}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\text{ for $k\neq i$}\\
\gamma_{i,j}\circ\delta^{v}_{k}&\cong\delta^{v}_{k}\circ\gamma_{i,j}\text{ for $k\neq j$}.\end{split}

Finally, we have the following special formulas for
\delta^{v,h}_{0}
and
\delta^{v,h}_{m+1,n+1}
:

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{1,0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{0}&\cong\delta^{h}_{0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\\
\gamma_{n+1,m}\circ\delta^{h}_{n+1}\circ\delta^{v}_{m+1}&\cong\delta^{v}_{m+1}\circ\delta^{h}_{n+1}.\end{split}

Proposition 2.6
.

The morphisms of the category
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
are generated by the morphisms
\sigma^{v,h}_{s}
,
\delta^{v,h}_{t}
and
\gamma_{i,j}
and the relations between them described in
2.5
.

Proof.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}}
the category with the same objects as
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
, but with morphisms generated by morphisms in
2.5
and relations between them, then there is a natural morphism
F:\widetilde{\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
and we need to prove that it is bijective on morphisms. To do this, we first describe a canonical presentation for a morphism in
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}}
. An arbitrary morphism
f
in
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}}
is given by a composition of a string of morphisms containing various
\sigma^{v,h}_{s}
,
\delta^{v,h}_{t}
and
\gamma_{i,j}
. First, observe that using
eq.

1
,
eq.

2
and
eq.

4
we can rewrite it as

(6)

\displaystyle f\cong\overline{\gamma}\circ\overline{i}\circ a^{h}\circ a^{v}\circ s^{h}\circ s^{v},

where
s^{h}
(resp.
s^{v}
) is given by a composition of various
\sigma^{h}_{i}
(resp.
\sigma^{v}_{j}
), the morphism
a^{h}
(resp.
a^{v}
) is a composition of
\delta^{h}_{k}
(resp.
\delta^{v}_{l}
), the morphism
\overline{i}
is a composition of morphisms of the form
\delta^{v,h}_{0}
and
\delta^{v,h}_{m+1,n+1}
and
\overline{\gamma}
is a composition of
\gamma_{i,j}
. This presentation is generally not unique. Assume that there are
n_{0}
terms of the form
\delta^{h}_{0}
in
\overline{i}
and
m_{0}
terms of the form
\delta^{v}_{m+1}
, then we claim that there is a decomposition of the morphism
f
of the form (
6
) in which there are no morphisms
\gamma_{i,j}
with
i<n_{0}
and
j>m_{0}
. Indeed, let
\overline{\gamma}\cong\gamma_{i_{N},j_{N}}\circ\gamma_{i_{N-1},j_{N-1}}\circ...\circ\gamma_{i_{0},j_{0}}
and let
l
be the minimal index for which
i_{l}<n_{0}
. It can only appear in
\overline{\gamma}
if the
(n_{0}-i_{l})
th morphism
\delta^{h}_{0}
in
\overline{i}
is followed by
\delta^{v}_{0}
. Then we can use the commutativity relations (
3
) and (
4
) as well as (
5
) to cancel them out. This reduces the number of
\gamma_{i,j}
with
i<n_{0}
by one, and we can continue the process by induction to completely get rid of them. The case of
\gamma_{i,j}
with
j>m_{0}
is similar. Finally, using
eq.

1
and
eq.

2
we can make it so that all the morphisms
\gamma_{i,j}
,
\delta_{k}
and
\sigma_{t}
are presented in the order of their coefficients, i.e. such that for example
s^{h}\cong\sigma_{t_{M}}\circ\sigma_{t_{M}-1}\circ...\circ\sigma_{t_{0}}
with
t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq...\leq t_{M}
. This decomposition is easily seen to be unique, and we call it
canonical
.

Now assume we have a morphism
F:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
. To prove the proposition it suffices to demonstrate that it admits a unique canonical decomposition as above. First, we can decompose it as
F_{i}\circ F_{s}
, where
F_{i}
is injective and
F_{s}
is surjective. Observe that a surjective morphism
F_{s}:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S_{0})
sends
S
onto
S_{0}\subset\mathrm{Sq}(S_{0})
, in particular it is easy to see that it is equal to a composition of morphisms of the form
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}
, so it follows that it admits a canonical decomposition. We can now assume
F
to be injective. Let
\widetilde{S}
be some maximal composable string of morphisms in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
that contains
F(S)
, then
F
factors as

\mathrm{Sq}(S)\xrightarrow{a_{\widetilde{S}}}\mathrm{Sq}(\widetilde{S})\xrightarrow{i_{\widetilde{S}}}\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime}),

where
i_{\widetilde{S}}
is a natural inclusion and
a_{\widetilde{S}}
sends
S
into
\widetilde{S}\subset\mathrm{Sq}(\widetilde{S})
. It is easy to see that
a_{\widetilde{S}}
is given by a composition of
\delta^{v,h}_{i}
and
i_{\widetilde{S}}
by a composition of
\gamma_{i,j}
. However, there are generally more than one
\widetilde{S}
that satisfy this condition. We endow the set of maximal composable chain in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
with a partial order for which
S_{1}<S_{2}
if
S_{1}
lies below
S_{2}
in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})\subset[n^{\prime}]\times[m^{\prime}]
. Let
F(0)=(l_{1},l_{2})
and
F(n,m)=(k_{1},k_{2})
, it is easy to see that for any
\widetilde{S}
the morphism
a_{\widetilde{S}}
can be further decomposed as

\mathrm{Sq}(S)\xrightarrow{b_{S_{0}}}\mathrm{Sq}(S_{0})\xrightarrow{j_{S_{0}}}\mathrm{Sq}(\widetilde{S}),

where
S_{0}
is the unique maximal composable chain in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
connecting
(l_{1},l_{2})
and
(k_{1},k_{2})
and containing
F(S)
,
j_{S_{0}}
is the natural inclusion and
b_{S_{0}}
is an injective morphism whose decomposition only contains
\delta^{h}_{i}
for
i\neq 0
and
\delta^{v}_{j}
for
j\neq m^{\prime}
.

It is now easy to see that the minimal (with respect to the partial order described above) maximal composable string
S_{1}
that contains
F(S)
can be described as follows: it starts at
(0,0)
and coincides with
S^{\prime}
until its horizontal coordinate reaches
l_{1}
, then it contains a vertical segment which continues until it hits
F(0)=(l_{1},l_{2})
, it then coincides with
S_{0}
until
(k_{1},k_{2})
, then it contains a horizontal segment until it hits
S^{\prime}
again and coincides with
S^{\prime}
until the endpoint
(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})
. In particular, it follows that
i_{S_{1}}
dies nor contain
\gamma_{i,j}
with
i<l_{1}
and
j>l_{2}
, so in particular
i_{S_{0}}\circ a_{S_{0}}\circ F_{s}
admits a canonical decomposition, and it is easy to see that it is uniquely determined by it.
∎

Example 2.7
.

The diagram below illustrates the canonical decomposition of a morphism
F:(2|1|1)\rightarrow(1|1|3|1|1|3)
such that
F(0,0)=F(1,0)=(2,2)
,
F(2,0)=(3,2)
,
F(2,1)=(3,4)
and
F(3,1)=(4,4)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(3,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(0,0)=F(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet.}

The dotted line depicts the composable sequence
S_{1}
in the notation of
2.6
. The decomposition itself is given by

F\cong\gamma_{3,2}\circ\gamma_{2,2}\circ\gamma_{4,3}\circ\gamma_{3,3}\circ\gamma_{4,4}\circ\gamma_{3,4}\circ\delta^{v}_{3}\circ\delta^{h}_{4}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{v}_{1}\circ\sigma^{h}_{0}.

Notation 2.8
.

We will call a morphism in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}

active
if its canonical decomposition only contains morphisms of the form
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}
and
\delta^{h}_{i}
for
i\neq 0
and
i\neq n+1
and
\delta^{v}_{k}
for
k\neq 0
and
k\neq m+1
. It follows from
eq.

1
and
eq.

2
that they are closed under composition and so form a subcategory, which we will denote by
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}
. We will call a morphism
inert
if its canonical decomposition only contains
\gamma_{i,j}
,
\delta^{v,h}_{0}
,
\delta^{v}_{m+1}
and
\delta^{h}_{n+1}
. It once again follows from
eq.

1
,
eq.

2
and
eq.

5
that they form a subcategory
k:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}
. We call an inert morphism an
inclusion
if its canonical decomposition does not contain
\gamma_{i,j}
, and we denote the subcategory of inclusions by
j:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}
. We will call an inert morphism a
permutation
if its canonical decomposition only contains morphisms of the form
\gamma_{i,j}
.

We will call an object of
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}

elementary
if the corresponding string
S
has length
l(S)\leq 1
, we denote by
i:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}
the full subcategory on elementary objects. For an object
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
we also denote by
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}
the category
S/i
. Denote by
\mathscr{E}{Fact}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{S})
on those
\mathscr{F}:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
for which

j^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong i_{*}i^{*}j^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Proposition 2.9
.

The active and inert morphisms form a factorization system on
\mathpzc{Fact}
. Moreover, the subcategory
\mathscr{E}{Fact}
gives the pair
(k:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Fact})
the structure of a theory with arities.

Proof.

The fact that active and inert morphisms form a factorization system follows immediately from
2.6
. To prove the second claim we need to show that the monad
k^{*}k_{!}
om
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{S})
preserves
\mathscr{E}
. For a given objects
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
denote by
\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)
the space of active morphisms with target
S
. Using the active/inert factorization, it can be considered as a functor
\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(-):\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
, although we would be more interested in its restriction to
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}
, which we would use the same notation for. Observe that it is a full subcategory of
k/S
on active morphisms
S^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow S
and moreover, since active and inert morphisms form a factorization system, we see that this subcategory is cofinal. It follows that

k^{*}k_{!}\mathscr{F}(S)\cong\underset{(S^{\prime}\rightarrow S)\in(k/s)}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S^{\prime})\cong\underset{(S^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow S)\in\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S^{\prime}).

Now let
S\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(i_{0}|j_{0}|...|n-i_{k}|m-j_{k})
, then it follows easily from the definition of active morphisms that

(7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)\cong\mathrm{Act}{Cat}([n])\times\mathrm{Act}{Cat}([m]).

Observe that we also have the following isomorphism

(8)

\displaystyle\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[i_{0}]/}\coprod_{(i_{0},0)}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[j_{0}]/}\coprod_{(i_{0},j_{0})}...\coprod_{(n-i_{k},m-j_{k})}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n-i_{k}]}\coprod_{(n,m-j_{k})}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m-j_{k}]}.

It follows from
eq.

7
,
eq.

8
and the basic properties of
\mathpzc{Cat}
that

\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)\cong\underset{(S\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{s/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(e).

We can also use
eq.

8
and the relative Segal condition for
\mathpzc{Cat}
to conclude that for any factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}a}

in which
i
is an inclusion and for every
\mathscr{F}:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
we have

\underset{(S^{\prime}\rightarrowtail e^{\prime})\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S^{\prime}/}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(e)\cong\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\underset{(i_{!}S^{\prime}\rightarrowtail e_{0})\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{i_{!}S^{\prime}/}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(e_{0}).

Putting everything together, we get for every
\mathscr{F}\in\mathscr{E}{Fact}

\displaystyle k^{*}k_{!}\mathscr{F}(S)\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(S^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow S)\in\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(e)}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{(S^{\prime}\rightarrowtail e^{\prime})\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S^{\prime}/}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(e)}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\underset{(i_{!}S^{\prime}\rightarrowtail e_{0})}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(e_{0})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\underset{(S\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(e)}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(i_{!}S^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}\underset{(i_{!}S\twoheadrightarrow e)\in\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(e)}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(i_{!}S)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(S\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\lim}k^{*}k_{!}\mathscr{F}(e)\cong i_{*}i^{*}k^{*}k_{!}\mathscr{F}(S),

where the isomorphism in the next to last row follows from
[
CH19
, Corollary 7.17]
.
∎

Notation 2.10
.

We will denote the category of models
\mathrm{Mod}{Fact}(\mathscr{S})
by
\mathrm{Fact}
.

Corollary 2.11
.

The morphisms
f
in
\mathpzc{Fact}
for which the inert part of the active/inert decomposition is a permutation form a subcategory, which we will denote by
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{a.p.}}
.

Proof.

To prove the claim it suffice to prove that
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{a.p.}}
is closed under composition. To prove it we need to demonstrate that if in a factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}a}

in
\mathpzc{Fact}
the morphism
i
is a permutation, then so is
a^{*}i
. Since every permutation and every active morphism admits a canonical decomposition, it would suffice to prove the claim for
i
of the from
\gamma_{i,j}
and for
a
equal to an elementary active morphism
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}
or
\delta^{v,h}_{i}
, however in this case the claim follows immediately from the identities described in
2.5
.
∎

}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.0268pt}{-13.26584pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Then this category admits an initial object.

Proof.

The required initial object is given by the inclusion
i:S_{i}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
of a minimal substring of
S^{\prime}
such that all the morphism in the image of
j
in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
admit a factorization which only includes morphisms from
S_{i}
. More explicitly it can be constructed as follows: assume that
S
(resp.
S^{\prime}
) is a maximal composable chain of morphisms in
[k]\times[l]
(resp. in
[k^{\prime}]\times[l^{\prime}]
), assume that
j(0,0)=(s,t)
and
j(k,l)=(u,v)
. We then define
(x,y)
(resp.
(z,w)
) to be the points in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
satisfying the following properties:

•

(x,y)
(resp.
(z,w)
) lies on
S^{\prime}
;

•

x=s
(resp.
w=v
);

•

there are no points in
\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
that satisfy the two conditions above and have lower (resp. higher) horizontal or vertical coordinates.

We then define
S_{i}
to be the substring of
S^{\prime}
lying between
(x,y)
and
(z,w)
. Since the image of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
under
j
lies in
\mathrm{Sq}(S_{i})\subset\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
by construction, we also have a morphism
j_{i}:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Sq}(S_{i})
such that
j\cong i\circ j_{i}
.
∎

Definition 2.13
.

We will call an inert morphism
j
in
\mathpzc{Fact}
a
covering
if the initial object of
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}/j
described in
2.12
is given by the identity.

Proposition 2.14
.

Coverings and inclusions form a factorization system in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}
.

Proof.

2.12
provides us with a decomposition
j\cong i\circ j_{i}
for an arbitrary inert morphism
j
, in which
i
is an inclusion and
j_{i}
is a covering, so to prove our claim it would suffice to prove that coverings form a subcategory of
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}
. Since identity morphisms are obviously coverings, it remains to show that they are closed under composition. So assume that we have a composable chain
S_{0}\overset{j_{0}}{\rightarrowtail}S_{1}\overset{j_{1}}{\rightarrowtail}S_{2}
in
\mathpzc{Fact}
in which both
J_{0}
and
J_{1}
are coverings, we need to prove that
j_{1}\circ j_{0}
is a covering as well. Assume that
\mathrm{Sq}(S_{i})\subset[k_{i}]\times[l_{i}]
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, then it follows from the definition that
j_{1}\circ j_{0}(0,0)
and
j_{1}(0,0)
(resp.
j_{1}\circ j_{0}(k_{0},l_{0})
and
j_{1}(k_{1},l_{1})
) have the same horizontal (resp. vertical) coordinate, and so it follows from the construction of
j_{i}
in
2.12
that if
j_{1}
is a covering, then so is
j_{1}\circ j_{0}
.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

The morphisms given by a composition of an active morphism and a covering do
not
form a subcategory of
\mathpzc{Fact}
. For example, both
\gamma_{1,0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{1}
and
\sigma^{v}_{0}
satisfy this property, however

\sigma^{v}_{0}\circ\gamma_{1,0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{1}\cong\sigma^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{1}\cong\delta^{h}_{1}

does not. It follows that the subcategory of inclusions in
\mathpzc{Fact}
is not a part of a factorization system.

Remark 2.16
.

Observe that
\mathrm{Fact}
is a localization of
\mathscr{P}(\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}})
at the set of morphisms

\underset{(S\rightarrowtail e)\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{S/}}{\mathrm{colim}}h_{e}\rightarrow h_{S},

where
S
is an arbitrary object of
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
. In particular. it is an accessible localization of a presheaf category and it follows from
[
Lur09
, Proposition 5.5.4.15.]
that it is presentable.

Proposition 2.17
.

There is a functor
h:\mathpzc{Cat}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}
(resp.
v:\mathpzc{Cat}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}
,
t:\mathpzc{Cat}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}
) that sends
[0]
to the unique string of length zero and sends
[1]
to the string
h
(resp.
v
,
vh
). Moreover, it induces a functor
h^{*}:\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
(resp.
v^{*}:\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
,
t^{*}:\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
).

Proof.

The construction of
h
and
v
is similar and we only define the first one. By definition,
h
sends
[n]
to the string
S^{h}_{n}
consisting of
n
consecutive horizontal morphisms. To define
h
on morphisms it suffices to describe
h(\delta_{i})
and
h(\sigma_{j})
, so we define

h(\sigma_{j})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sigma^{h}_{j},h(\delta_{i})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\delta^{h}_{i}.

To prove that this is indeed a functor it suffices to demonstrate that
h(\delta_{i})
and
h(\sigma_{j})
satisfy simplicial identities, however this follows directly from
eq.

1
. To prove the second claim, observe that for every
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Fact}
we have

\mathscr{F}(S^{h}_{n})\cong\overbrace{\mathscr{F}(h)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}...\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h)}^{\text{$n$ times}}

and so the Segal condition for
h^{*}\mathscr{F}
follows.

The case of
t
is somewhat more difficult. We define
t([n])
to be the string

S^{t}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\overbrace{vhvh...vh}^{\text{$n$ times}}.

A morphism of the form
\sigma_{j}
is then sent to
\sigma^{v}_{j}\circ\sigma^{h}_{j}
, the morphisms
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{n+1}
to
\delta^{v}_{0}\circ\delta^{h}_{0}
and
\delta^{h}_{n+1}\circ\delta^{v}_{n+1}
respectively and the morphisms of the form
\delta_{i}
for
i\neq 0,n+1
to the composition

vh...vh...vh\xrightarrow{\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}}vh...vvhh...vh\xrightarrow{\gamma_{i,i}}vh...vhvh...vh.

The simplicial identities then follow relatively easily from the relations in
2.5
, we will only demonstrate the least trivial ones:

\displaystyle t(\sigma_{i}\circ\delta_{i})\cong\sigma^{v}_{i}\circ\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\gamma_{i,i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\cong\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{v}_{i}\circ\gamma_{i,i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\cong\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\sigma^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\cong\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle t(\sigma_{i-1}\circ\delta_{i})\cong\sigma^{v}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{h}_{i-1}\circ\gamma_{i,i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\cong\sigma^{v}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\cong\sigma^{v}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{h}_{i}\circ\delta^{h}_{i}\circ\delta^{v}_{i}\cong\mathrm{id}.

To prove the second claim in this case, observe that for every
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Fact}
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(S^{t}_{n})\cong

\displaystyle\overbrace{\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}...\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h)}^{\text{$n$ times}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\overbrace{(\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h))\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}(\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h))\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}...\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}(\mathscr{F}(v)\times_{\mathscr{F}(*)}\mathscr{F}(h))}^{\text{$n$ times}},

and so the Segal condition for
t^{*}\mathscr{F}
follows.
∎

Theorem 2.18
.

There is an equivalence between the category
\mathrm{Fact}
and the category whose objects are categories with factorization systems in the sense of
2.1
and morphisms are morphisms preserving the factorization system. Moreover, for an object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Fact}
the Segal spaces
h^{*}\mathscr{F}
and
v^{*}\mathscr{F}
can be identified under the above equivalence with the horizontal and vertical subcategories of
\mathscr{F}
respectively in the sense of
2.1
.

Proof.

We temporarily denote by
\widetilde{\mathrm{Fact}}
the category described in the statement of the proposition. Observe that, for given an object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Fact}
, the Segal space
t^{*}\mathscr{F}
of
2.17
admits a factorization system by definition. This defines a functor
F:\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{Fact}}
.

Now assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a category with a factorization system
(\mathscr{H},\mathscr{V})
. We can consider it as a functor

(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{act}}:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}):(i_{0}|j_{0}|...|n-i_{k}|m-i_{k})\mapsto\mathscr{H}_{i_{0}}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{V}_{j_{0}}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}...\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{H}_{n-i_{k}}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{V}_{m-j_{k}}

for which every morphism of the form
\sigma^{v,h}_{j}
or
\delta^{v,h}_{i}
acts as a corresponding unit or multiplication map for
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{H}
respectively. By the unique factorization property any morphism
f:S\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
that sends horizontal segments of
S
to horizontal morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
and vertical segments to vertical morphisms admits a unique extension to the morphism
\widetilde{f}:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{Fact}}
(using the same construction as in
lemma

2.4
). Observe that all inert morphisms
i:S\rightarrowtail S^{\prime}
in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
induce an inclusion of categories
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
. This allows us to consider
\mathscr{C}
as a functor
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{int}}:\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
that sends every inert morphism
i
as above to the morphism

(i^{*}:\mathrm{Hom}(S^{\prime},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(S,\mathscr{C})):(f:S^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\mapsto(S\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)\overset{i}{\hookrightarrow}\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}}\mathscr{C}).

We now need to show that
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{act}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{int}}
glue together to define a functor
\mathscr{C}:\mathpzc{Fact}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. For that it suffices to show that for any factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{a}}

in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
we have
\widetilde{i}^{*}\circ\widetilde{a}^{*}\cong a^{*}\circ i^{*}
. Using
2.6
we can express each of the morphism in this diagram as a composition of the elementary morphisms described in
2.5
, so it suffices to prove this statement under the assumption that
\widetilde{a}
and
\widetilde{i}
are elementary morphisms. In this case the claim reduces to checking the relations described in the
2.5
. Observe that if
\widetilde{i}
is an elementary inert morphism of the form
\sigma^{v,h}_{0}
or
\sigma^{v,h}_{m+1,n+1}
, then the relations we need to check reduce to simplicial identities, which hold since
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{V}
were assumed to be subcategories of
\mathscr{C}
. It remains to prove the claim for
\widetilde{i}=\gamma_{i,j}
. In this case we are reduced to checking the equivalences in
eq.

4
. Observe that the third and fourth as well as the seventh and eights equivalences in
eq.

4
are trivial. Proving that
\sigma^{h,*}_{i}\cong\sigma^{h,*}_{i-1}\circ\gamma^{*}_{j,i}
amounts to proving that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

is a factorization square for any vertical morphism
v
, which is obvious. The isomorphism
\sigma^{v,*}_{j}\cong\sigma^{v,*}_{j}\circ\gamma^{*}_{j,i}
reduces to an analogous statement for the horizontal subcategory. Similarly, the proving isomorphism
\gamma^{*}_{i+1,j}\circ\gamma^{*}_{i,j}\circ\delta^{h,*}_{i}\cong\delta^{h,*}_{i}\circ\gamma^{*}_{i,j}
amounts to proving that for a pair of factorization squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

in
\mathscr{C}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}_{2}\circ h^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}\circ h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

is also a factorization square, which follows easily from the uniqueness of factorization. Thus we have defined a functor
G:\widetilde{\mathrm{Fact}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fact}
.

Finally, the fact that
F
and
G
are inverse to each other as well as the last claim of the proposition follow easily from the construction.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

The morphism
t:\mathpzc{Cat}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}
of
2.17
is cofinal.

Proof.

To prove the claim we need to show that for every object
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
the category
(S/t)
is contractible. The objects of this category are given by morphisms
t([n])\xrightarrow{f}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
in
\mathrm{Fact}
. Since every morphism in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
admits a unique decomposition
f\cong h^{\prime}\circ v^{\prime}
with a horizontal morphism
h^{\prime}
and a vertical morphism
v^{\prime}
, we see that a morphism
t([n])\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
in
\mathrm{Fact}
can be identified with a morphism
[n]\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S)
in
\mathrm{Cat}
. Every morphism
[n]\xrightarrow{f}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
admits a unique factorization

[n]\overset{f_{s}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[n_{i}]\xrightarrow{f_{i}}\mathrm{Sq}(S)

in which
f_{s}
is a surjective morphism and
f_{i}
is injective. It follows that the full subcategory
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}\hookrightarrow(S/t)
on injective morphisms is cofinal. This in turn implies that the classifying spaces for both categories are homotopy equivalent, so it now suffices to prove that
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
is contractible.

An object of
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
can be identified with a sequence of distinct points
(a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})
in
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
with non-decreasing horizontal and vertical coordinates. There is a unique morphism from
(a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})
to
(b_{1},b_{2},...,b_{m})
if
\{a_{1},...,a_{n}\}
is a subset of
\{b_{1},...,b_{m}\}
. Denote by
(S/t)^{\mathrm{act}}
the full subcategory of
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
on those sequences that contain both initial and final objects of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
. For an arbitrary object
\overline{a}
of
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
denote by
L\overline{a}
the sequence obtained from
\overline{a}
by adding the final and initial objects if they do not belong to
\overline{a}
already. It is obvious from the description of
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
above that
L
is a left adjoint to the inclusion
(S/t)^{\mathrm{act}}\hookrightarrow(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
. In particular, the classifying spaces of
(S/t)^{\mathrm{inj}}
and
(S/t)^{\mathrm{act}}
are also equivalent. Finally, observe that
(S/t)^{\mathrm{act}}
contains an initial object given by the sequence containing only the initial object and the final object of
\mathrm{Sq}(S)
, so
(S/t)^{\mathrm{act}}
is contractible.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Both
h_{!}
and
v_{!}
of
2.17
restrict to functors

h_{!},v_{!}:\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fact}.

Proof.

We will only prove the statement for
h
leaving the other case to the reader. By definition for
\mathscr{C}\in\mathrm{Cat}
we have

h_{!}\mathscr{C}(S)\cong\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow h([n])\in(h/S)}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}([n]).

Observe that every morphism
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow h(n)
uniquely factors as

\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow h([n_{S}])\xrightarrow{h(f)}[n],

where
[n_{S}]
is an interval obtained from
S
by contracting all vertical morphisms and
f
is a morphism in
\mathpzc{Cat}
. It follows that

h_{!}\mathscr{C}(S)\cong\mathscr{C}([n_{S}]).

The fact that it satisfies the Segal condition now follows elementary.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

It follows from
lemma

2.20
that we have a morphism

(h_{!},v_{!}):\mathrm{Cat}\times\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fact}.

Explicitly, for a pair
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\in\mathrm{Cat}\times\mathrm{Cat}
and
\mathrm{Sq}(S)\subset[n]\times[m]
we have

(h_{!},v_{!})(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})(S)\cong\mathscr{C}_{n}\times\mathscr{D}_{m}.

This factorization system can be identified with a natural factorization system on
\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
having horizontal morphisms of the form
(f,\mathrm{id}_{d})
and vertical morphisms of the form
(\mathrm{id}_{c},g)
, and so we will often implicitly identify
\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
with an object of
\mathrm{Fact}
.

3.
Lax functors and distributive laws

The goal of this section is to define lax functors from categories with factorization systems and prove their basic properties. We define this notion in a few stages. First, we define the notion of a bicategory with a factorization system in
definition

3.2
and prove that the category
2-\mathrm{Fact}
of bicategories with factorization systems is also equivalent to a category of models of a certain theory in
3.3
. We then construct an endofunctor
\mathscr{L}
of
2-\mathrm{Fact}
in
3.5
. The actual construction is somewhat complicated, but roughly speaking
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{F}
is obtained from
\mathscr{F}
by adding 2-morphisms
\gamma_{h,v}:v\circ h\rightarrow\widetilde{h}\circ\widetilde{v}
for every factorization
v\circ h\cong\widetilde{h}\circ\widetilde{v}
in
\mathscr{F}
. Our next step is the construction of
L^{\mathrm{lax}}:\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Fact}
in
3.19
. This functor adds the ”compositor” and ”unitor” 2-morphisms to all morphisms and objects in the vertical and horizontal subcategories of
\mathscr{F}
. Finally, we define
\mathscr{L}^{\mathrm{lax}}
to be the composition
\mathscr{L}L^{\mathrm{lax}}
and define a lax functor from
\mathscr{F}
to a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
to be a morphism
\mathscr{L}^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
.

The main result of this section is
3.31
, which gives an explicit description of
\mathscr{L}^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathrm{Sq}(S)
for objects
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
. Unfortunately, the proof is quite technical and takes up most of the section. Near the end of the section we use it to define a distributive law between monads in
definition

3.32
.

Construction 3.1
.

Denote by
2-\mathpzc{Fact}
the category
\mathpzc{Fact}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
. Observe that it has a natural active/inert factorization for which a morphism is active (resp. inert) if both of its components in
\mathpzc{Fact}
and
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
are active (resp. inert) . We also declare a morphism an inclusion if its projection to
\mathpzc{Fact}
is an inclusion and its projection to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is inert, we denote by
j:2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}\hookrightarrow 2-\mathpzc{Fact}
the subcategory with the same objects and inclusions as morphisms. Finally, we declare an object to be elementary if it is of the form
(v,[i])
for
i\in\{0,1\}
,
(h,[j])
for
j\in\{0,1\}
or
(*,[0])
, we denote by
i:2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow 2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{incl}}
the inclusion of the full subcategory on elementary objects. Finally, we denote by
2-\mathrm{Fact}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathpzc{Fact},\mathscr{S})
on those
\mathscr{F}
for which

j^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong i_{*}i^{*}j^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Definition 3.2
.

For a given bicategory
\mathscr{B}
and a positive integer
n
denote by
\mathrm{Ar}^{n,\mathrm{id}}(\mathscr{B})
the Segal space
i_{n}^{*}\mathscr{B}
, where
i_{n}:\{n\}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is the natural inclusion. In other words, the objects of
\mathrm{Ar}^{n,\mathrm{id}}(\mathscr{B})
are given by composable sequences

b_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}b_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}b_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n}}b_{n}

and morphisms by diagrams

\hbox to182.51pt{\vbox to42.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 91.25626pt\lower-22.34401pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Assume that
\mathscr{B}
has a pair of subcategories
(\mathscr{H},\mathscr{V})
, denote by
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{Fact}}(\mathscr{H},\mathscr{V})
the subcategory of
\mathrm{Ar}^{2,\mathrm{id}}(\mathscr{B})
on objects of the form
h\circ v
with
v\in\mathscr{V}
and
h\in\mathscr{H}
and morphisms of the form
\gamma_{h}*\gamma_{v}
with
\gamma_{h}\in\mathscr{H}
and
\gamma_{v}\in\mathscr{V}
. Observe that the composition of morphisms induces a functor

m:\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{Fact}}(\mathscr{H},\mathscr{V})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{id}}(\mathscr{B}).

We will say that
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{V}
form a
factorization system
on
\mathscr{B}
if
m
is an isomorphism. If
\mathscr{E}
is another bicategory with factorization system
(\mathscr{L},\mathscr{M})
and
F:\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
is a functor, we say that
F
preserves the factorization system if it sends
\mathscr{H}
to
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{V}
to
\mathscr{M}
. We will denote by
\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
the subcategory of
2-\mathrm{Cat}
on bicategories with factorization systems and functors that preserve the factorization systems.

Proposition 3.3
.

There is an equivalence

\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}\xrightarrow{\sim}2-\mathrm{Fact}.

Proof.

First, we define a functor
G:2-\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
. The construction is similar to
2.17
: we denote by
\widetilde{t}
the morphism

(t,\mathrm{id}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Fact}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\cong 2-\mathpzc{Fact}.

It is easy to check that
\widetilde{t}^{*}
restricts to a functor
2-\mathrm{Fact}\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Cat}
and moreover for any
\mathscr{F}\in 2-\mathrm{Fact}
we have an isomorphism of Segal spaces

\widetilde{t}^{*}\mathscr{F}_{1}\cong\mathscr{F}_{v}\times_{\mathscr{F}_{0}}\mathscr{F}_{h},

where
\mathscr{F}_{h}
(resp.
\mathscr{F}_{v}
) is a Segal space given by the restriction of
\mathscr{F}
to
\{h\}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\subset 2-\mathrm{Fact}
(resp.
\{v\}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\subset 2-\mathrm{Fact}
). It follows that
\widetilde{t}^{*}\mathscr{F}
is an object of
\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
.

We now construct a functor
H:\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Fact}
. First, for any object
(S,l)
of
2-\mathpzc{Fact}
, where
S
is a string with
n
horizontal and
m
vertical segments, denote by
\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)
the the full subcategory of
\theta_{n,l}\times\theta_{m.l}
(where
\theta_{n,l}
denotes the category
\theta(n;l,l,...,l)
in the notation of
3.11
) on objects that lie above
S
, these bicategories can then be considered as objects of
\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
. Observe that every morphism
f:(S,l)\rightarrow(S^{\prime},l^{\prime})
in
2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
induces a morphism
\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, in particular for every
\mathscr{F}\in\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
we can define
H(\mathscr{F}):2-\mathpzc{Fact}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
by

H(\mathscr{F}):(S,l)\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}}(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l),\mathscr{F}).

Observe that every morphism
\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
in
\widetilde{2-\mathrm{Fact}}
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
(S,l)\subset\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)
(where
(S,l)
denotes the full subcategory on objects belonging to
S\subset[n]\times[m]
), it follows easily from this that
H(\mathscr{F})
satisfies the Segal condition for
2-\mathrm{Fact}
. Finally, it is elementary to check that
H
and
G
are inverse to each other.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

A similar argument to
lemma

2.20
shows that for morphisms

\widetilde{h}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(h,\mathrm{id}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow 2-\mathpzc{Fact}

and

\widetilde{v}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(v,\mathrm{id}):\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow 2-\mathpzc{Fact}

the morphisms
\widetilde{h}_{!}
and
\widetilde{v}_{!}
restrict to morphisms from
2-\mathrm{Cat}
to
2-\mathrm{Fact}
. In particular, just like in
remark

2.21
we can consider

(\widetilde{h}_{!},\widetilde{v}_{!}):2-\mathrm{Cat}\times 2-\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Fact}

which endows a product
\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{E}
of bicategories with the natural factorization system.

Construction 3.5
.

Given an object
(S,l)\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}
, denote by
\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S,l)
the full subcategory of
\theta_{n,l}\otimes\theta_{m,l}
on objects that lie above
S
considered as a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,m)
in
[n]\times[m]
. It is easy to see that every morphism
(S,l)\rightarrow(S^{\prime},l^{\prime})
induces a functor
\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, so
\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(-)
defines a functor
2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Cat}
. We can consider this as a correspondence
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}:2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}\nrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
by viewing
2-\mathrm{Cat}
as a full subcategory of
\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)
on the presheaves satisfying the Segal condition. Finally, we denote by
\mathscr{L}
the composition

2-\mathrm{Fact}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}}\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)\xrightarrow{L}2-\mathrm{Cat},

where
L
denotes the left adjoint to the fully faithful inclusion
2-\mathrm{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)
(which exists since
2-\mathrm{Cat}
is an accessible localization of
\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)
).

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
\mathscr{F}\in 2-\mathrm{Fact}
and
\mathscr{B}\in 2-\mathrm{Cat}
we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})\cong\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S),\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

First, observe that we have the following isomorphism in
\mathscr{P}(2-\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}})
:

\mathscr{F}\cong\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S)\times h_{\mathrm{Sq}(S)}.

Now the result follows from the following sequence of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(L\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)}(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)}(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S)\times h_{\mathrm{Sq}(S)},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)}(\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(S)\times\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S),\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\Delta\times\Delta)}(\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S),\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(\mathrm{Sq}(S,l)\rightarrow\mathscr{F})\in 2-\mathpzc{Fact}/\mathscr{F}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{Sq}^{\mathrm{lax}}(S),\mathscr{B}).

∎

Notation 3.7
.

For a pair of twofold Segal spaces
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})\in(2-\mathrm{Cat})^{\times 2}
denote by
\mathscr{B}\otimes\mathscr{E}
the twofold Segal space
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{E})
, where we consider
\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{E}
to be an object of
2-\mathrm{Fact}
through the use of
remark

3.4
.

Corollary 3.8
.

For
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})\in(2-\mathrm{Cat})^{\times 2}
we have an isomorphism

\mathscr{B}\otimes\mathscr{E}\cong\underset{(\theta_{n,m}\rightarrow\mathscr{B})\in 2-\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{B}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{(\theta_{s,t}\rightarrow\mathscr{E})\in 2-\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{E}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{B}_{n,m}\times\mathscr{E}_{s,t})\times(\theta_{n,m}\otimes\theta_{s,t}).

Proof.

Indeed, this follows from the definition of
\mathscr{B}\otimes\mathscr{E}
,
lemma

3.6
and the fact that
2-\mathrm{Cat}\times 2-\mathrm{Cat}
is generated under colimits by
\theta_{n,m}\times\theta_{s,t}
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Since
\mathscr{B}\otimes\mathscr{E}
commutes with colimits in both variables by
3.8
and coincides with the usual Gray product (constructed for
\infty
-categories in
[
GHL20
]
) on
(\theta_{n,m},\theta_{s,t})
, it follows that it coincides with the usual Gray tensor product for all pairs
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})
.

Proposition 3.10
.

For any pair
(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})\in(2-\mathrm{Cat})^{\times 2}
and any
\mathscr{T}\in 2-\mathrm{Cat}
there is an isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B}\otimes\mathscr{E},\mathscr{T})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B},\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{T}))

in the notation of
[
Kos21
, Definition 4.6.]
.

Proof.

First, observe that it follows from
3.8
that it suffices to prove the statement for pairs of the form
(\theta_{n,m},\theta_{s,t})
. Moreover, it follows from the colimit decomposition of
[
Kos21
, Lemma 4.5.]
that we can further restrict our attention to
(n,m,s,t)\in\{0,1\}^{4}
, in which case the result becomes elementary.
∎

Construction 3.11
.

Recall from
[
Hau18
]
the following description of the category
\Theta_{2}
: its objects are given by pairs
([n],a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})
where all
a_{i}
are non-negative integers, a morphism from
([n],a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})
to
([m],b_{1},b_{2},...,b_{m})
is given by a morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
in
\Delta
together with morphisms
f_{i,j}:[a_{i}]\rightarrow[b_{j}]
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
f(i)\leq j\leq f(i+1)
. To every such object we can associate a bicategory
\theta(n;a_{1},...,a_{n})
with
n+1
objects such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta(n,a_{1},...,a_{n})}(i,j)\cong[a_{i}]\times[a_{i+1}]\times...\times[a_{j}]
if
i\leq j
and is empty otherwise. Now assume we have an object
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
that contains
n
horizontal segments and
m
vertical segments together with non-negative integers
(h_{1},...,h_{n})
and
(v_{1},...,v_{m})
, to this data we can associate a bicategory
\mathrm{Sq}(S;h_{1},...,h_{n};v_{1},...,v_{m})
which is by definition the full subcategory of
\theta(n;h_{1},...,h_{n})\times\theta(m,v_{1},...,v_{m})
on objects lying above
S
viewed as a path from
(0,0)
to
(n,m)
. Observe that
\mathrm{Sq}(S;h_{1},...,h_{n};v_{1},...,v_{m})
has a natural structure of an object of
2-\mathrm{Fact}
, we will denote by
(\Theta{Fact}_{2})^{\mathrm{op}}
the full subcategory of
2-\mathrm{Fact}
on objects of the form
\mathrm{Sq}(S;h_{1},...,h_{n};v_{1},...,v_{m})
. If
S=e_{1}e_{2}...e_{n+m}
, where
e\in\{h,v\}
, we will also sometimes denote
(S;h_{1},...,h_{n};v_{1},...,v_{m})
by
(S;l_{1},l_{2},...,l_{n+m})
, where
l_{i}
is the number corresponding to the segment
e_{k}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

The category
\Theta{Fact}_{2}
admits an active/inert factorization system.

Proof.

First, observe that a morphism

f:\mathrm{Sq}(S;h_{1},...,h_{n};v_{1},...,v_{m})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime};h^{\prime}_{1},...,h^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}};v^{\prime}_{1},...,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})

in
(\Theta{Fact}_{2})^{\mathrm{op}}
can equivalently be described by the data of a morphism
\overline{f}:\mathrm{Sq}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(S^{\prime})
in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
together with morphisms
f_{i,j}:[h_{i}]\rightarrow[h^{\prime}_{j}]
for
0\leq i\leq n
and
\overline{f}_{h}(i)\leq j\leq\overline{f}_{h}(i+1)
and
g_{k,t}:[v_{k}]\rightarrow[v_{t}]
for
0\leq k\leq m
and
\overline{f}_{v}(k)\leq t\leq\overline{f}_{v}(k+1)
(where
f_{h,v}
denotes the horizontal and vertical projections of
f
). We declare a morphism
f
to be inert (resp. active) if
\overline{f}
is inert (resp. active) as a morphism in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
and all morphisms
f_{i,j}
and
g_{s,t}
are inert (resp. active) as morphisms in
\Delta
. The claim now follows from the existence of the active/inert factorization systems in
\Delta
and
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
∎

Notation 3.13
.

We will denote by
k:(\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\Theta_{2}{Fact}
the inclusion of the subcategory containing inert morphisms. We will call an inert morphism
(i;i_{1},...,i_{n};j_{1},...,j_{m})
an inclusion if the morphism
i
in
\mathpzc{Fact}
is an inclusion, we denote by
j:(\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{incl}}\hookrightarrow(\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{int}}
the subcategory of inclusions. We declare an object
(e;s)
to be elementary if
e\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}
and
s\in\{0,1\}
and denote by
i:(\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow(\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{incl}}
the full subcategory on elementary objects. Finally, we denote by
\mathscr{E}_{\Theta_{2}{Fact}}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\Theta_{2}{Fact})^{\mathrm{int}},\mathscr{S})
on those functors
\mathscr{F}:(\Theta{Fact}_{2})^{\mathrm{int}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
for which

j^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong i_{*}i^{*}j^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Lemma 3.14
.

For a given
S\in\mathpzc{Fact}
denote by
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/S}
the full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Fact}_{/S}
on active morphisms, then we have

\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/S}\cong\underset{(S\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/S}}{\lim}\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/e}.

Proof.

First, observe that
\mathrm{Act}_{\mathpzc{Fact}}(S)
is the underlying space of
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/S}
and we have already proved the claim for
\mathrm{Act}{Fact}(S)
in
2.9
. It would now suffice to provide an isomorphism on morphisms. Observe that a morphism in
\mathpzc{Fact}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/S}
is by definition a diagram of the form
